## RESEARCH FOR DEVELOPMENT (R4D) LEARNING SERIES                     ISSN 2522-7076

10 [SOURCE: Page_2]

Shifting Gender Relations in Agriculture and Irrigation in the Nepal Tarai-Madhesh Sujeet Karn, Fraser Sugden, Krishna Kumar Sah, Jenisha Maharjan, Tula Narayan Shah and Floriane Clement [SOURCE: Page_2]

CGIAR Research Program on Water, Land and Ecosystems The CGIAR Research Program on Water, Land and Ecosystems (WLE) is a global research-for-development program connecting partners to deliver sustainable agriculture solutions that enhance our natural resources – and the lives of people that rely on them. WLE brings together 11 CGIAR centers, the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO), the RUAF Global Partnership, and national, regional and international partners to deliver solutions that change agriculture from a driver of environmental degradation to part of the solution. WLE is led by the International Water Management Institute (IWMI) and partners as part of CGIAR, a global research partnership for a food-secure future. [SOURCE: Page_3]

About the Research for Development (R4D) Learning Series The WLE Research for Development (R4D) Learning Series is one of the main publication channels of the program. Papers within the series present new thinking, ideas and perspectives from WLE research with a focus on the implications for development and research into use. Papers are based on finalized research or emerging research results. In both instances, papers are peer-reviewed and findings are based on sound scientific evidence and data, though these might be incomplete at the time of publication. [SOURCE: Page_3]

The series features findings from WLE research that emphasize a healthy functioning ecosystem as being a prerequisite to sustainable intensification, resilience of food systems and human well-being. The series brings together multi-disciplinary research, global synthesis and findings that have implications for development practitioners and decision makers at various levels. [SOURCE: Page_3]

Acknowledgments                                                                Project [SOURCE: Page_6]

I would like to express my sincere gratitude to the International Water        This research study was undertaken as part of the project G$_7$: Poverty Management Institute (IWMI) for giving me the assignment of conducting         squares and gender circles: Unraveling agriculture gaps, challenges ethnographic research as part of the Poverty squares and gender                and opportunities in the Eastern Gangetic Plains (Bangladesh, India, circles: Unraveling agricultural gaps, challenges and opportunities            Nepal). It was part of WLE’s focal region program that ran from 2014 in the Eastern Gangetic Plains of Nepal project. In particular, I would        to 2016. like to thank Dr. Fraser Sugden, former head of the Gender, Youth and Inclusivity core theme of the CGIAR Research Program on Water,             Collaborators Land and Ecosystems (WLE), and Dr. Floriane Clement, former Nepal country representative and gender focal point for IWMI, for providing advice and support while conducting this research, which would not have been possible without their continuous feedback and assistance. The support provided by Professor Deepa Joshi, Gender, Youth and International Water Management Institute Inclusion (GYI) Lead Specialist, IWMI, Colombo, Sri Lanka (formerly (IWMI) Assistant Professor, Wageningen University, The Netherlands) is gratefully acknowledged. [SOURCE: Page_6]

I would also like to thank staff of the IWMI office in Nepal for the support provided during my visit. They were extremely helpful in arranging             Nepal Madesh Foundation, Lalitpur, Nepal finances and other logistics throughout this research study.                   (https://nemaf.org.np/) [SOURCE: Page_6]

During this research, many people from the districts under study – Sunsari and Siraha – provided help and support in various ways, including farmers who participated in conducting the research. I am Donors extremely grateful to all informants, including the people of Ekamba and Amaduwa village development committees (VDCs) of Sunsari district as well as Bhagwanpur and Fulkahakati VDCs of Siraha district; irrigation office staff in both these districts; Groundwater Resources Development Board (GWRDB) office staff in Siraha district; and Irrigation and Water This research was carried out as part of the CGIAR Research Program Resources Management Project (IWRMP) staff in Sunsari district for on Water, Land and Ecosystems (WLE) and supported by Funders their warmth and hospitality, and for repeatedly sharing their stories with contributing to the CGIAR Trust Fund (https://www.cgiar.org/funders/). us and patiently answering all our questions. Thanks also go to Rosan Chaudhari (Gate Keeper, IWMI), Dinesh Mahato (Mobilizer, GWRDB), Pradip Singh Danuwwar (Farmer’s Association), Yogendra P. Yadav (Head Teacher, Amaduwa Secondary School) and Sunil Giri (Mobilizer, GWRDB) who assisted us at the field sites and introduced us to various people who later served as informants. [SOURCE: Page_6]

Special thanks go to the team from the Nepal Madhesh Foundation (NEMAF), in particular Krishan Kumar Sah and Jenisha Maharjan, who conducted field surveys and collected qualitative data. They also arranged local logistics in the field and meticulously contributed to the overall research work. Subha Narayan Yadav is also thanked for the support provided during fieldwork. [SOURCE: Page_6]

Finally, I would like to thank Tula Narayan Shah for his overall guidance and support provided to the NEMAF team to conduct research in the field. [SOURCE: Page_6]

Sujeet Karn Kathmandu, Nepal 2020 [SOURCE: Page_6]

As a result, many women’s Madhesh, have a deeply inequitable social structure.              well-being has decreased as a consequence of male out- Poverty is deeply entrenched as a consequence of class            migration, but some women have also become empowered inequalities, legacies from a feudal tax hierarchy and            in new ways, taking up enterprise opportunities and active inequitable landlord–tenant relations. Deep-rooted gender         roles in water user groups. disparities are endemic. However, in Sunsari district, women’s roles within water In recent years, growing male out-migration has resulted          user associations for canals were found to be marginal in new patterns of gendered vulnerability, with women             and largely tokenistic. This is despite women’s critical role increasingly tasked with managing and accessing irrigation.       in agricultural production and their dependence on access While these women generally experience economic                   to canal water. In Siraha district, women from poorer insecurity and increased work burdens, their experiences          households without their own pumps faced challenges in are highly dependent on their class, ethnicity and religion.      accessing groundwater. However, the biggest challenges In addition, women’s access to credit and state subsidies,        pertaining to groundwater access were identified as as well as their capacity for engaging in enterprises and         institutional weaknesses. other activities, also determine the risks and opportunities they face.                                                        At the level of policy and external development interventions, the authors identified a dominating narrative on women’s Understanding the realities of women in the Tarai-Madhesh,        limited participation in agriculture being a result of ‘social including the complex interrelations between the social,          norms’. Public irrigation agencies used this myth to absolve economic, historical and cultural factors that shape their        themselves of the responsibility for ensuring gender equality lives, is a prerequisite for effective agricultural development   in program implementation. It was found to divert attention programs and strategies.                                          away from gender discrepancies within public irrigation agencies, thereby preventing a reflexive and critical effort Therefore, this report explores how women perceive                on greater gender equality within the bureaucracy. participation and empowerment vis-a-vis access to water and other agricultural resources. The authors draw on             The authors conclude that strengthening equitable irrigation fieldwork carried out over 6 months in 2015 and 2016. The         user groups, along with capacity building for farmers and work focused on communities in two districts of the Tarai-        program implementers, is critical for improving women’s Madhesh – Sunsari and Siraha – selected for this study            access to irrigation and overall well-being. Women should due to their dependence on access to canal water and              be ensured meaningful, rather than tokenistic, participation, groundwater irrigation, respectively.                             including leadership roles. It is recommended to link irrigation user groups to other income-generation schemes The data indicate that gendered vulnerability is indeed           and let cooperation across groups facilitate access to intricately connected with other axes of difference, such         better credit, finance and agricultural inputs. ## SHIFTING GENDER RELATIONS IN AGRICULTURE AND IRRIGATION IN THE NEPAL TARAI-MADHESH

INTRODUCTION The Eastern Gangetic Plains (EGP), spread across                 cultural marginalization as a region (Gaige 1970), and an the Tarai regions of Nepal, North Bengal and Bihar in            extractive relationship with Kathmandu (Regmi 1976). The India, and northern Bangladesh, present a paradoxical            region today has a deeply inequitable social structure. situation in relation to agriculture, poverty and inequality.    Central to the reproduction of poverty in Tarai-Madhesh are The region – home to approximately 600 million of the            the deeply entrenched class inequalities, and a legacy of world’s poorest people – is characterized by fragmented          the old feudal tax collection hierarchy, with a high incidence landholdings, widespread landlessness, poor investments          of inequitable landlord–tenant relations. Land tenure and and infrastructure, out-migration, and inequalities based        ownership is one of the most significant challenges facing on class, caste, ethnicity and gender (Lahiri-Dutt 2012).        agriculture in the Tarai-Madhesh, and indeed much of the EGP (Sugden 2013, 2017). Photo: Fraser Sugden [SOURCE: Page_39]

CGIAR Research Program on Water, Land and Ecosystems The CGIAR Research Program on Water, Land and Ecosystems (WLE) is a global research-for-development program connecting partners to deliver sustainable agriculture solutions that enhance our natural resources – and the lives of people that rely on them. WLE brings together 11 CGIAR centers, the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO), the RUAF Global Partnership, and national, regional and international partners to deliver solutions that change agriculture from a driver of environmental degradation to part of the solution. WLE is led by the International Water Management Institute (IWMI) and partners as part of CGIAR, a global research partnership for a food-secure future. [SOURCE: Page_39]

About the Research for Development (R4D) Learning Series The WLE Research for Development (R4D) Learning Series is one of the main publication channels of the program. Papers within the series present new thinking, ideas and perspectives from WLE research with a focus on the implications for development and research into use. Papers are based on finalized research or emerging research results. In both instances, papers are peer-reviewed and findings are based on sound scientific evidence and data, though these might be incomplete at the time of publication. [SOURCE: Page_39]

The series features findings from WLE research that emphasize a healthy functioning ecosystem as being a prerequisite to sustainable intensification, resilience of food systems and human well-being. The series brings together multi-disciplinary research, global synthesis and findings that have implications for development practitioners and decision makers at various levels. [SOURCE: Page_39]
